Traumatic events? In my whole life? Goodness, no, I've had a very blessed life, really. Nothing I would call traumatic. 

I mean, there was the time my sister Evelyn lost her favorite brooch at the state fair. Oh, she was absolutely devastated. It was this beautiful cameo pin our grandmother gave her, with a delicate little gold rim. We searched everywhere for it—the pie-tasting pavilion, the Ferris wheel line, even near those giant pumpkins they give blue ribbons to. Have you ever seen one of those pumpkins? They're practically the size of a Volkswagen! I don't know how they get them that big. Probably some special fertilizer. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, uses coffee grounds on his hydrangeas, and let me tell you, the blooms are as big as dinner plates. A gorgeous shade of blue, too. He says it's the acidity in the coffee. I tried it once with my rose bushes, but I think I used the wrong kind of coffee. Arthur used to buy that cheap instant stuff in the big glass jars. It tasted like hot dirt, if you ask me, but he drank it every single morning with exactly two sugars. Never cream. He said cream ruined the flavor. Flavor! As if that instant powder had any flavor to begin with. We used to keep the empty jars to store nails and screws out in the garage. Arthur had a whole wall of them, all labeled with that little embossing machine that punches the white letters into the plastic tape. Click, click, click... it made such a satisfying sound. 

But... oh, what were you asking me? Traumatic events? No, no, nothing like that. Just a very ordinary, happy life.

I just sit down at that table, and I look at it all, and I have absolutely no idea which thing to do first. I just stare at it, and before I know it, the whole day is just gone. Poof. Just like that. And I haven't done a single thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks other than organizing your kitchen table?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just the table. It’s... well, anything like that. When there’s a form to fill out, or those long insurance papers to read... oh, Medicare sends so much mail. Just the other day, our mailman—Gary, he's been on our route for fifteen years now, his daughter just had twins, a boy and a girl, which is just such a blessing. Anyway, Gary brought this thick envelope from the supplemental insurance. \n\nAnd I know I need to read it, or like when I have to put together the program schedule for the senior center... I volunteer there on Tuesdays, though last Tuesday we had a terrible plumbing issue in the main hall so we had to cancel bingo. Such a shame, Martha had already bought all the prizes. But yes, when I try to sit down to do the schedule or read those insurance forms, I just... I keep finding little reasons to put it off. I'll go water the African violets, or wipe down the counters again, or check the weather on the television. My head just feels completely worn out before I even start. It's like my brain is exhausted just looking at the envelope, so I just wander off and do something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you need to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, remembering things... yes, it's just awful lately. It really is. I’ll promise my daughter I’ll run an errand for her—she lives just two towns over, in a lovely little neighborhood, though the property taxes there are just highway robbery if you ask me. They just raised them again to build a new high school stadium. I don't know why they need artificial turf, grass was perfectly fine when we were growing up, wasn't it? \n\nBut yes, I'll completely forget the errand. Or I'll leave a bill sitting right there under a stack of mail and just forget it exists. I’ve missed returning phone calls, too. And more than once, I’ve realized way too late that I never called to confirm one of my husband’s appointments. He gets so frustrated with me, and I don't blame him. The doctor's office has that automated calling system now, you know, the robot voice? It always calls right during Jeopardy, and Alex Trebek—well, Ken Jennings now, he's doing a fine job, really, but it's not the same—anyway, the robot voice talks so fast, and I just forget to press one to confirm, and then it just completely slips my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Oh, distracted... yes, yes, all the time. It's like I have no filter for what's going on around me. Just the other day I was trying to write out the grocery list, and my husband had the television on in the living room. He watches those old Westerns, you know, Gunsmoke and Bonanza. They have a whole channel for it now, just all day long, horses and cowboys. I remember when we only had three channels, and you had to get up to turn the dial! My father used to make me be the remote control when I was a little girl. \n\nBut anyway, if the TV is on in the next room, or even at the senior center... if I'm trying to plan the activities and two people start talking near me, my mind just completely follows their conversation. I'll be listening to them talk about their grandchildren or their garden—Helen grows the most beautiful hydrangeas, she puts pennies in the soil to make them blue, did you know that works? It has something to do with the acidity. But yes, I'll just start listening to them, and then I look down at my paper and I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning. It just flies right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're faced with these tasks and can't seem to focus?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed? Oh, well... I'm not really sure if I'd call it overwhelmed, exactly. Or anxious. I mean, maybe a little? But mostly it's just... well, my husband, he gets anxious. Oh, terribly anxious, especially when he watches the news. He sits in his recliner—we bought it at that furniture store downtown, the one that used to be a movie theater back in the fifties. We saw *Singin' in the Rain* there on our first date. Such a wonderful movie, they just don't make them like that anymore, with the real dancing. Gene Kelly was just magnificent. \n\nBut yes, he watches the news and gets all worked up about the economy and the price of eggs. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's just astonishing. I used to bake an angel food cake every Sunday, takes twelve egg whites, you know. Twelve! But now I just make a simple pound cake instead. It's easier anyway, and it keeps longer in that nice glass dome my sister gave me for our anniversary. Our fortieth, I think it was. Or maybe the forty-fifth? \n\nAnyway... what were we talking about? Oh, feeling anxious. I don't know, I just... I haven't really thought about it that way. I just know I can't seem to get the things done that I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, I suppose... I don't really know if I'd use that word. I just feel so mixed up sometimes. It's more like a fog, really. Like when the weather changes and that thick fog rolls in off the lake. We used to take the kids to the lake every summer, rented a little cabin with a screened-in porch. The mosquitoes were just terrible, though. We'd burn those little green coils, you know the ones? They smelled awful, but they kept the bugs away. My son used to try and catch the fireflies in a mason jar. \n\nBut anxious... I don't think so. I just feel bad that I can't seem to keep things straight anymore. It's just frustrating, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? Oh, goodness, yes. I just can't seem to stay still, even when I really ought to. Like when I'm trying to sit politely at the planning meetings for the senior center. I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot under the table. Or I'll be clicking my pen—click, click, click. It's one of those nice gel pens, you know, the ones that write so smoothly? I bought a whole pack of them at the pharmacy last week. The pharmacist there, David, he is just the sweetest young man. He always asks about our golden retriever, Buster. Buster is getting so gray around the muzzle now, just like my husband! We've had him since he was just a little puppy, fit right in a shoebox. \n\nBut yes, at the meetings, I just shift around in my chair so much that the other ladies actually stop and look over at me. Martha gave me quite the glare last Tuesday because I was moving around so much while she was talking. She's very strict about Robert's Rules of Order, you know. She used to be a high school principal, and she still treats us all like we're teenagers in detention if we make too much noise. But I just can't help it, my body just wants to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving around or fidgeting even when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about those meetings, I certainly don't do it on purpose! Goodness no. I'll just be sitting there, trying so hard to be polite and listen, and suddenly I realize my foot is going a mile a minute, or I'm rubbing my hands together again. I don't even know I'm doing it until I catch myself. Or until someone gives me a look, like Martha does. \n\nIt's like my body just has a mind of its own. I was actually thinking about taking up knitting to give my hands something to do when I have to sit still. My grandmother was a beautiful knitter. She made these gorgeous afghans, all in pastel colors. We had this lovely peach-colored one draped over the back of the sofa for years and years. Oh, it was so warm in the winter... the winters used to be so much colder back then, didn't they? We'd get snow piled all the way up to the porch steps. \n\nBut yes, I definitely don't try to fidget. I just sort of... realize I'm shifting around in my chair or messing with my pen after I've already been doing it for heaven knows how long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Frustrated, certainly, like I mentioned before with the fog and not keeping things straight, but not down in the dumps. I actually try to keep a very cheerful disposition. My mother always used to say, \"A smile is the best umbrella on a rainy day.\" She was such a wise woman, my mother. She used to bake the most wonderful apple pies. The secret, she said, was in the crust—she used half butter and half shortening. And she always put a little sprinkle of nutmeg in with the cinnamon. I tried to make it for Thanksgiving last year, but it just didn't taste the same. We had Thanksgiving at my daughter's house this time. She has this beautiful big dining table that seats twelve. It's solid oak, very heavy. It took three men just to get it through her front door when she bought it! \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel sad. I have my family, I have Buster... I'm just terribly annoyed with myself for being so scattered all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid tasks because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. If it's something where I have to really concentrate... like when there's a form to fill out, or those awful insurance papers to read, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies at the center. I just look at the pile and my head feels completely worn out before I even start! \n\nSo I keep finding little reasons to put it off. I'll tell myself, \"Oh, I really must water the African violets first.\" I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill, you know. The purple one is just blooming beautifully right now. I got the original cutting from my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, oh, must be fifteen years ago now. She moved down to Florida shortly after that to be closer to her daughter. Tampa, I think? Or maybe it was Boca Raton. Her daughter had just had a baby boy. Goodness, that boy must be driving by now! They grow up so fast, don't they? It feels like just yesterday my own children were running through the sprinklers in the backyard. We used to have one of those yellow slip-and-slides, and the grass would get so muddy... \n\nBut yes, the paperwork and the schedules. I just avoid them like the plague because it feels like climbing a mountain just thinking about starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Details? Oh, I suppose... well, I don't really know if I'd say I have trouble with them. I just don't always notice them, I guess? Like, I'll read a recipe and completely miss that it said \"teaspoon\" instead of \"tablespoon.\" I did that just last week with a batch of bran muffins. Oh, they were terribly salty! I had to throw the whole lot out for the birds. The blue jays didn't seem to mind, though. We have a pair of them that nest in the big oak tree out front every spring. They are so noisy, squawking away at all hours of the morning! Buster, my dog, he just sits at the window and barks back at them. He's a terrier mix, you know, very spunky. We got him from the shelter about five years ago after my husband passed. The man at the shelter said he was a \"schnoodle,\" a schnauzer and poodle mix, but I think he's got some Jack Russell in him, the way he jumps. He can jump right up onto the back of the sofa from a standstill! \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, details. I'm not sure I really have trouble with them, I just... I suppose I gloss over things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you worry about not being able to do them correctly?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think it's because I'm worried about doing them wrong. I mean, I know how to do things properly, I've been running a household for nearly fifty years! It's just the... the energy it takes to keep it all straight in my head. But no, not because I'm afraid of making a mistake. I've never been a perfectionist, really. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she was a perfectionist. Everything in her house had to be just so. She used to iron her sheets! Can you imagine? Ironing bedsheets! She had this heavy old GE iron, the kind with the thick cord wrapped in that fabric stuff. It weighed a ton. She actually burned a hole right through one of her good pillowcases once because she left it sitting too long while she went to answer the telephone. It was one of those old rotary phones, you know, the black ones that hung on the kitchen wall with the extra-long cord so you could stretch it all the way to the stove while you were cooking. We used to talk for hours on Sundays. The rates were cheaper on Sundays back then. I remember when long distance was a real luxury, you had to wait until after 9 PM to call out of state. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't avoid things because I'm worried about getting them wrong. I'm perfectly capable, it's just getting my brain to cooperate and focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? Oh, heavens no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I have my moments, of course, especially since Arthur passed, but mostly I'm quite content. I have my garden, and the ladies at the center, and Buster keeping me on my toes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of empty, I did open my pantry yesterday and realized I was completely out of peach preserves. I usually make them myself every August when the peaches are in season. We used to drive out to this lovely little orchard off Route 9—Miller's Farm, it was called. Old Mr. Miller had the sweetest peaches you ever tasted. He always wore these faded denim overalls and a straw hat that looked like it had seen better days. He had a golden retriever named Rusty who would follow him everywhere, just trotting along behind the tractor. I wonder if that farm is still there? So many of those family places got bought up by developers to build those big, identical houses. You know the ones, they all have those tiny little yards where you can barely fit a patio set, let alone a proper garden. I couldn't imagine living without a garden. Where would I put my hydrangeas? \n\nBut no, I don't feel sad or empty most of the time. I stay busy enough, even if I don't always get around to the things I'm supposed to be doing!\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Stressful or traumatic? No, no, nothing like that. It's been quite peaceful around here lately, really. Just the usual day-to-day things. \n\nWell, I suppose the most stressful thing recently was when the mail carrier started coming at a different time. It used to be you could set your watch by him—11:15 sharp, every morning. It was a lovely man named Gary, always had a pocket full of those little milk bone treats for Buster. But then Gary retired last month. He moved down to Florida, somewhere near Sarasota, I think. His daughter lives down there, and she just had twins. A boy and a girl! Imagine having twins at her age, she must be nearly forty. My cousin Betty had twins back in '78, and let me tell you, it was a handful. She used to put them in this massive double stroller that took up the entire sidewalk. You couldn't even get past her if you were walking the other way, you'd have to step right off the curb into the street. And the streets back then weren't paved as nicely as they are now, all those cobblestones downtown. I twisted my ankle on one of those cobblestones once, right in front of the old Woolworth's. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, nothing traumatic or overly stressful. Just the mail being late!\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Completing tasks? Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say I have trouble finishing things once I actually get my hands on them. I mean, I knitted a whole scarf just last week! I used this lovely periwinkle yarn I found on sale at the craft shop. That shop used to be over by the old cinema, you know, the one that had the big neon marquee with the missing letters? We used to go to the matinees there for fifty cents when I was younger. They always played those wonderful old Hitchcock films. \n\nRear Window was my absolute favorite. Grace Kelly's dresses in that movie were just exquisite. Women really dressed up back then, even just to go to the grocery store. You'd never catch my mother leaving the house without her pearls and a matching handbag. She had this one black patent leather purse that always smelled like peppermint because she kept those little individually wrapped candies at the bottom. The ones with the red and white stripes? They get so sticky if you leave them in there too long. I actually found one in an old coat pocket once and it was practically glued to the lining. It was a terrible shame because it was a beautiful wool coat, a real heavy winter one that Arthur bought me for our fifth anniversary. We went up to the mountains that year, and it snowed three feet... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh! Finishing things. No, if I start a task, I usually get it done just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention on tasks?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Or trouble paying attention? Oh, I don't think so. I've always been quite particular about things, really. \n\nAlthough, I suppose I did put the television remote in the refrigerator yesterday. But that's just because I was holding it when the phone rang, and I was trying to get out the milk for my tea at the same time. The phone ring is so loud now, I had my grandson turn it up because I kept missing calls when I was out in the garden. He's so good with all those gadgets, you know. He's studying computer science at the university. It's amazing what they can do with computers these days. When I was taking a few classes at the community college—oh, this must have been back in the late seventies—we had to use typewriters. And if you made a mistake, you had to use that little white correction fluid. It smelled awful, like chemicals, and it took forever to dry. If you typed over it too soon, it would make a terrible mess on the ribbon. I had a lovely pale blue Smith Corona typewriter. My father bought it for me when I graduated high school. He was always so proud of my typing speed. He used to time me with his pocket watch. It was a beautiful gold watch that belonged to his grandfather, passed down through the family. It had this intricate engraving on the back of a train, because his grandfather worked for the railroad. The Union Pacific, I believe it was. They used to travel all across the country...\n\nBut, oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? What was the question? Something about making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say I do that often. Just the usual little slips anyone might have.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life that might be contributing to your current difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? In my whole life? Goodness, no, I've had a very blessed life, really. Nothing I would call traumatic. \n\nI mean, there was the time my sister Evelyn lost her favorite brooch at the state fair. Oh, she was absolutely devastated. It was this beautiful cameo pin our grandmother gave her, with a delicate little gold rim. We searched everywhere for it—the pie-tasting pavilion, the Ferris wheel line, even near those giant pumpkins they give blue ribbons to. Have you ever seen one of those pumpkins? They're practically the size of a Volkswagen! I don't know how they get them that big. Probably some special fertilizer. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, uses coffee grounds on his hydrangeas, and let me tell you, the blooms are as big as dinner plates. A gorgeous shade of blue, too. He says it's the acidity in the coffee. I tried it once with my rose bushes, but I think I used the wrong kind of coffee. Arthur used to buy that cheap instant stuff in the big glass jars. It tasted like hot dirt, if you ask me, but he drank it every single morning with exactly two sugars. Never cream. He said cream ruined the flavor. Flavor! As if that instant powder had any flavor to begin with. We used to keep the empty jars to store nails and screws out in the garage. Arthur had a whole wall of them, all labeled with that little embossing machine that punches the white letters into the plastic tape. Click, click, click... it made such a satisfying sound. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking me? Traumatic events? No, no, nothing like that. Just a very ordinary, happy life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I just sort of... realize I'm shifting around in my chair or messing with my pen after I've already been doing it for heaven knows how long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Frustrated, certainly, like I mentioned before with the fog and not keeping things straight, but not down in the dumps. I actually try to keep a very cheerful disposition. My mother always used to say, \"A smile is the best umbrella on a rainy day.\" She was such a wise woman, my mother. She used to bake the most wonderful apple pies. The secret, she said, was in the crust—she used half butter and half shortening. And she always put a little sprinkle of nutmeg in with the cinnamon. I tried to make it for Thanksgiving last year, but it just didn't taste the same. We had Thanksgiving at my daughter's house this time. She has this beautiful big dining table that seats twelve. It's solid oak, very heavy. It took three men just to get it through her front door when she bought it! \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel sad. I have my family, I have Buster... I'm just terribly annoyed with myself for being so scattered all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid tasks because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. If it's something where I have to really concentrate... like when there's a form to fill out, or those awful insurance papers to read, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies at the center. I just look at the pile and my head feels completely worn out before I even start! \n\nSo I keep finding little reasons to put it off. I'll tell myself, \"Oh, I really must water the African violets first.\" I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill, you know. The purple one is just blooming beautifully right now. I got the original cutting from my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, oh, must be fifteen years ago now. She moved down to Florida shortly after that to be closer to her daughter. Tampa, I think? Or maybe it was Boca Raton. Her daughter had just had a baby boy. Goodness, that boy must be driving by now! They grow up so fast, don't they? It feels like just yesterday my own children were running through the sprinklers in the backyard. We used to have one of those yellow slip-and-slides, and the grass would get so muddy... \n\nBut yes, the paperwork and the schedules. I just avoid them like the plague because it feels like climbing a mountain just thinking about starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Details? Oh, I suppose... well, I don't really know if I'd say I have trouble with them. I just don't always notice them, I guess? Like, I'll read a recipe and completely miss that it said \"teaspoon\" instead of \"tablespoon.\" I did that just last week with a batch of bran muffins. Oh, they were terribly salty! I had to throw the whole lot out for the birds. The blue jays didn't seem to mind, though. We have a pair of them that nest in the big oak tree out front every spring. They are so noisy, squawking away at all hours of the morning! Buster, my dog, he just sits at the window and barks back at them. He's a terrier mix, you know, very spunky. We got him from the shelter about five years ago after my husband passed. The man at the shelter said he was a \"schnoodle,\" a schnauzer and poodle mix, but I think he's got some Jack Russell in him, the way he jumps. He can jump right up onto the back of the sofa from a standstill! \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, details. I'm not sure I really have trouble with them, I just... I suppose I gloss over things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you worry about not being able to do them correctly?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think it's because I'm worried about doing them wrong. I mean, I know how to do things properly, I've been running a household for nearly fifty years! It's just the... the energy it takes to keep it all straight in my head. But no, not because I'm afraid of making a mistake. I've never been a perfectionist, really. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she was a perfectionist. Everything in her house had to be just so. She used to iron her sheets! Can you imagine? Ironing bedsheets! She had this heavy old GE iron, the kind with the thick cord wrapped in that fabric stuff. It weighed a ton. She actually burned a hole right through one of her good pillowcases once because she left it sitting too long while she went to answer the telephone. It was one of those old rotary phones, you know, the black ones that hung on the kitchen wall with the extra-long cord so you could stretch it all the way to the stove while you were cooking. We used to talk for hours on Sundays. The rates were cheaper on Sundays back then. I remember when long distance was a real luxury, you had to wait until after 9 PM to call out of state. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't avoid things because I'm worried about getting them wrong. I'm perfectly capable, it's just getting my brain to cooperate and focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? Oh, heavens no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I have my moments, of course, especially since Arthur passed, but mostly I'm quite content. I have my garden, and the ladies at the center, and Buster keeping me on my toes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of empty, I did open my pantry yesterday and realized I was completely out of peach preserves. I usually make them myself every August when the peaches are in season. We used to drive out to this lovely little orchard off Route 9—Miller's Farm, it was called. Old Mr. Miller had the sweetest peaches you ever tasted. He always wore these faded denim overalls and a straw hat that looked like it had seen better days. He had a golden retriever named Rusty who would follow him everywhere, just trotting along behind the tractor. I wonder if that farm is still there? So many of those family places got bought up by developers to build those big, identical houses. You know the ones, they all have those tiny little yards where you can barely fit a patio set, let alone a proper garden. I couldn't imagine living without a garden. Where would I put my hydrangeas? \n\nBut no, I don't feel sad or empty most of the time. I stay busy enough, even if I don't always get around to the things I'm supposed to be doing!\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Stressful or traumatic? No, no, nothing like that. It's been quite peaceful around here lately, really. Just the usual day-to-day things. \n\nWell, I suppose the most stressful thing recently was when the mail carrier started coming at a different time. It used to be you could set your watch by him—11:15 sharp, every morning. It was a lovely man named Gary, always had a pocket full of those little milk bone treats for Buster. But then Gary retired last month. He moved down to Florida, somewhere near Sarasota, I think. His daughter lives down there, and she just had twins. A boy and a girl! Imagine having twins at her age, she must be nearly forty. My cousin Betty had twins back in '78, and let me tell you, it was a handful. She used to put them in this massive double stroller that took up the entire sidewalk. You couldn't even get past her if you were walking the other way, you'd have to step right off the curb into the street. And the streets back then weren't paved as nicely as they are now, all those cobblestones downtown. I twisted my ankle on one of those cobblestones once, right in front of the old Woolworth's. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, nothing traumatic or overly stressful. Just the mail being late!\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Completing tasks? Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say I have trouble finishing things once I actually get my hands on them. I mean, I knitted a whole scarf just last week! I used this lovely periwinkle yarn I found on sale at the craft shop. That shop used to be over by the old cinema, you know, the one that had the big neon marquee with the missing letters? We used to go to the matinees there for fifty cents when I was younger. They always played those wonderful old Hitchcock films. \n\nRear Window was my absolute favorite. Grace Kelly's dresses in that movie were just exquisite. Women really dressed up back then, even just to go to the grocery store. You'd never catch my mother leaving the house without her pearls and a matching handbag. She had this one black patent leather purse that always smelled like peppermint because she kept those little individually wrapped candies at the bottom. The ones with the red and white stripes? They get so sticky if you leave them in there too long. I actually found one in an old coat pocket once and it was practically glued to the lining. It was a terrible shame because it was a beautiful wool coat, a real heavy winter one that Arthur bought me for our fifth anniversary. We went up to the mountains that year, and it snowed three feet... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh! Finishing things. No, if I start a task, I usually get it done just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention on tasks?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Or trouble paying attention? Oh, I don't think so. I've always been quite particular about things, really. \n\nAlthough, I suppose I did put the television remote in the refrigerator yesterday. But that's just because I was holding it when the phone rang, and I was trying to get out the milk for my tea at the same time. The phone ring is so loud now, I had my grandson turn it up because I kept missing calls when I was out in the garden. He's so good with all those gadgets, you know. He's studying computer science at the university. It's amazing what they can do with computers these days. When I was taking a few classes at the community college—oh, this must have been back in the late seventies—we had to use typewriters. And if you made a mistake, you had to use that little white correction fluid. It smelled awful, like chemicals, and it took forever to dry. If you typed over it too soon, it would make a terrible mess on the ribbon. I had a lovely pale blue Smith Corona typewriter. My father bought it for me when I graduated high school. He was always so proud of my typing speed. He used to time me with his pocket watch. It was a beautiful gold watch that belonged to his grandfather, passed down through the family. It had this intricate engraving on the back of a train, because his grandfather worked for the railroad. The Union Pacific, I believe it was. They used to travel all across the country...\n\nBut, oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? What was the question? Something about making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say I do that often. Just the usual little slips anyone might have.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life that might be contributing to your current difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? In my whole life? Goodness, no, I've had a very blessed life, really. Nothing I would call traumatic. \n\nI mean, there was the time my sister Evelyn lost her favorite brooch at the state fair. Oh, she was absolutely devastated. It was this beautiful cameo pin our grandmother gave her, with a delicate little gold rim. We searched everywhere for it—the pie-tasting pavilion, the Ferris wheel line, even near those giant pumpkins they give blue ribbons to. Have you ever seen one of those pumpkins? They're practically the size of a Volkswagen! I don't know how they get them that big. Probably some special fertilizer. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, uses coffee grounds on his hydrangeas, and let me tell you, the blooms are as big as dinner plates. A gorgeous shade of blue, too. He says it's the acidity in the coffee. I tried it once with my rose bushes, but I think I used the wrong kind of coffee. Arthur used to buy that cheap instant stuff in the big glass jars. It tasted like hot dirt, if you ask me, but he drank it every single morning with exactly two sugars. Never cream. He said cream ruined the flavor. Flavor! As if that instant powder had any flavor to begin with. We used to keep the empty jars to store nails and screws out in the garage. Arthur had a whole wall of them, all labeled with that little embossing machine that punches the white letters into the plastic tape. Click, click, click... it made such a satisfying sound. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking me? Traumatic events? No, no, nothing like that. Just a very ordinary, happy life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Throughout my life? Oh, well... yes, I suppose it has always been this way, really. Even when I was a little girl, my teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was a bright student but very careless. They said I was always forgetting my books or leaving my homework at home, and they constantly had to remind me to finish my seatwork before I was allowed to go outside for recess. 

Recess was always my favorite part of the day, of course. We used to play hopscotch on the blacktop behind the school. My best friend Sally had this special piece of pink chalk she brought from home, and she'd draw the most perfect, straight lines for the squares. She was very artistic, Sally. She ended up going to art school in Chicago. I always wanted to visit her there, but the train tickets were so expensive back then. And the trains! They were so loud and smoky. I remember taking the train to visit my aunt in St. Louis once, and I wore a brand new white linen dress. By the time we arrived, there were little black soot spots all over the collar. My aunt tried to get them out with lemon juice and sunshine—she swore by lemon juice for everything. She even put it in her hair to make it lighter in the summer. It smelled wonderful, like a fresh pie...

Oh, dear, I've done it again, haven't I? What were you asking me? Oh, right, if it's new. No, no, it's been like this since I was in grade school, I'm afraid.

I'm not really sure if I'd call it overwhelmed, exactly. Or anxious. I mean, maybe a little? But mostly it's just... well, my husband, he gets anxious. Oh, terribly anxious, especially when he watches the news. He sits in his recliner—we bought it at that furniture store downtown, the one that used to be a movie theater back in the fifties. We saw *Singin' in the Rain* there on our first date. Such a wonderful movie, they just don't make them like that anymore, with the real dancing. Gene Kelly was just magnificent. \n\nBut yes, he watches the news and gets all worked up about the economy and the price of eggs. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's just astonishing. I used to bake an angel food cake every Sunday, takes twelve egg whites, you know. Twelve! But now I just make a simple pound cake instead. It's easier anyway, and it keeps longer in that nice glass dome my sister gave me for our anniversary. Our fortieth, I think it was. Or maybe the forty-fifth? \n\nAnyway... what were we talking about? Oh, feeling anxious. I don't know, I just... I haven't really thought about it that way. I just know I can't seem to get the things done that I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, I suppose... I don't really know if I'd use that word. I just feel so mixed up sometimes. It's more like a fog, really. Like when the weather changes and that thick fog rolls in off the lake. We used to take the kids to the lake every summer, rented a little cabin with a screened-in porch. The mosquitoes were just terrible, though. We'd burn those little green coils, you know the ones? They smelled awful, but they kept the bugs away. My son used to try and catch the fireflies in a mason jar. \n\nBut anxious... I don't think so. I just feel bad that I can't seem to keep things straight anymore. It's just frustrating, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? Oh, goodness, yes. I just can't seem to stay still, even when I really ought to. Like when I'm trying to sit politely at the planning meetings for the senior center. I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot under the table. Or I'll be clicking my pen—click, click, click. It's one of those nice gel pens, you know, the ones that write so smoothly? I bought a whole pack of them at the pharmacy last week. The pharmacist there, David, he is just the sweetest young man. He always asks about our golden retriever, Buster. Buster is getting so gray around the muzzle now, just like my husband! We've had him since he was just a little puppy, fit right in a shoebox. \n\nBut yes, at the meetings, I just shift around in my chair so much that the other ladies actually stop and look over at me. Martha gave me quite the glare last Tuesday because I was moving around so much while she was talking. She's very strict about Robert's Rules of Order, you know. She used to be a high school principal, and she still treats us all like we're teenagers in detention if we make too much noise. But I just can't help it, my body just wants to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving around or fidgeting even when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about those meetings, I certainly don't do it on purpose! Goodness no. I'll just be sitting there, trying so hard to be polite and listen, and suddenly I realize my foot is going a mile a minute, or I'm rubbing my hands together again. I don't even know I'm doing it until I catch myself. Or until someone gives me a look, like Martha does. \n\nIt's like my body just has a mind of its own. I was actually thinking about taking up knitting to give my hands something to do when I have to sit still. My grandmother was a beautiful knitter. She made these gorgeous afghans, all in pastel colors. We had this lovely peach-colored one draped over the back of the sofa for years and years. Oh, it was so warm in the winter... the winters used to be so much colder back then, didn't they? We'd get snow piled all the way up to the porch steps. \n\nBut yes, I definitely don't try to fidget. I just sort of... realize I'm shifting around in my chair or messing with my pen after I've already been doing it for heaven knows how long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Frustrated, certainly, like I mentioned before with the fog and not keeping things straight, but not down in the dumps. I actually try to keep a very cheerful disposition. My mother always used to say, \"A smile is the best umbrella on a rainy day.\" She was such a wise woman, my mother. She used to bake the most wonderful apple pies. The secret, she said, was in the crust—she used half butter and half shortening. And she always put a little sprinkle of nutmeg in with the cinnamon. I tried to make it for Thanksgiving last year, but it just didn't taste the same. We had Thanksgiving at my daughter's house this time. She has this beautiful big dining table that seats twelve. It's solid oak, very heavy. It took three men just to get it through her front door when she bought it! \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel sad. I have my family, I have Buster... I'm just terribly annoyed with myself for being so scattered all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid tasks because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. If it's something where I have to really concentrate... like when there's a form to fill out, or those awful insurance papers to read, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies at the center. I just look at the pile and my head feels completely worn out before I even start! \n\nSo I keep finding little reasons to put it off. I just avoid them like the plague because it feels like climbing a mountain just thinking about starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Details? Oh, I suppose... well, I don't really know if I'd say I have trouble with them. I just don't always notice them, I guess? Like, I'll read a recipe and completely miss that it said \"teaspoon\" instead of \"tablespoon.\" I did that just last week with a batch of bran muffins. Oh, they were terribly salty! I had to throw the whole lot out for the birds. The blue jays didn't seem to mind, though. We have a pair of them that nest in the big oak tree out front every spring. They are so noisy, squawking away at all hours of the morning! Buster, my dog, he just sits at the window and barks back at them. He's a terrier mix, you know, very spunky. We got him from the shelter about five years ago after my husband passed. The man at the shelter said he was a \"schnoodle,\" a schnauzer and poodle mix, but I think he's got some Jack Russell in him, the way he jumps. He can jump right up onto the back of the sofa from a standstill! \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, details. I'm not sure I really have trouble with them, I just... I suppose I gloss over things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you worry about not being able to do them correctly?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think it's because I'm worried about doing them wrong. I mean, I know how to do things properly, I've been running a household for nearly fifty years! It's just the... the energy it takes to keep it all straight in my head. But no, not because I'm afraid of making a mistake. I've never been a perfectionist, really. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she was a perfectionist. Everything in her house had to be just so. She used to iron her sheets! Can you imagine? Ironing bedsheets! She had this heavy old GE iron, the kind with the thick cord wrapped in that fabric stuff. It weighed a ton. She actually burned a hole right through one of her good pillowcases once because she left it sitting too long while she went to answer the telephone. It was one of those old rotary phones, you know, the black ones that hung on the kitchen wall with the extra-long cord so you could stretch it all the way to the stove while you were cooking. We used to talk for hours on Sundays. The rates were cheaper on Sundays back then. I remember when long distance was a real luxury, you had to wait until after 9 PM to call out of state. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't avoid things because I'm worried about getting them wrong. I'm perfectly capable, it's just getting my brain to cooperate and focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? Oh, heavens no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I have my moments, of course, especially since Arthur passed, but mostly I'm quite content. I have my garden, and the ladies at the center, and Buster keeping me on my toes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of empty, I did open my pantry yesterday and realized I was completely out of peach preserves. I usually make them myself every August when the peaches are in season. We used to drive out to this lovely little orchard off Route 9—Miller's Farm, it was called. Old Mr. Miller had the sweetest peaches you ever tasted. He always wore these faded denim overalls and a straw hat that looked like it had seen better days. He had a golden retriever named Rusty who would follow him everywhere, just trotting along behind the tractor. I wonder if that farm is still there? So many of those family places got bought up by developers to build those big, identical houses. You know the ones, they all have those tiny little yards where you can barely fit a patio set, let alone a proper garden. I couldn't imagine living without a garden. Where would I put my hydrangeas? \n\nBut no, I don't feel sad or empty most of the time. I stay busy enough, even if I don't always get around to the things I'm supposed to be doing!\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Stressful or traumatic? No, no, nothing like that. It's been quite peaceful around here lately, really. Just the usual day-to-day things. \n\nWell, I suppose the most stressful thing recently was when the mail carrier started coming at a different time. It used to be you could set your watch by him—11:15 sharp, every morning. It was a lovely man named Gary, always had a pocket full of those little milk bone treats for Buster. But then Gary retired last month. He moved down to Florida, somewhere near Sarasota, I think. His daughter lives down there, and she just had twins. A boy and a girl! Imagine having twins at her age, she must be nearly forty. My cousin Betty had twins back in '78, and let me tell you, it was a handful. She used to put them in this massive double stroller that took up the entire sidewalk. You couldn't even get past her if you were walking the other way, you'd have to step right off the curb into the street. And the streets back then weren't paved as nicely as they are now, all those cobblestones downtown. I twisted my ankle on one of those cobblestones once, right in front of the old Woolworth's. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, nothing traumatic or overly stressful. Just the mail being late!\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Completing tasks? Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say I have trouble finishing things once I actually get my hands on them. I mean, I knitted a whole scarf just last week! I used this lovely periwinkle yarn I found on sale at the craft shop. That shop used to be over by the old cinema, you know, the one that had the big neon marquee with the missing letters? We used to go to the matinees there for fifty cents when I was younger. They always played those wonderful old Hitchcock films. \n\nRear Window was my absolute favorite. Grace Kelly's dresses in that movie were just exquisite. Women really dressed up back then, even just to go to the grocery store. You'd never catch my mother leaving the house without her pearls and a matching handbag. She had this one black patent leather purse that always smelled like peppermint because she kept those little individually wrapped candies at the bottom. The ones with the red and white stripes? They get so sticky if you leave them in there too long. I actually found one in an old coat pocket once and it was practically glued to the lining. It was a terrible shame because it was a beautiful wool coat, a real heavy winter one that Arthur bought me for our fifth anniversary. We went up to the mountains that year, and it snowed three feet... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh! Finishing things. No, if I start a task, I usually get it done just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention on tasks?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Or trouble paying attention? Oh, I don't think so. I've always been quite particular about things, really. \n\nAlthough, I suppose I did put the television remote in the refrigerator yesterday. But that's just because I was holding it when the phone rang, and I was trying to get out the milk for my tea at the same time. The phone ring is so loud now, I had my grandson turn it up because I kept missing calls when I was out in the garden. He's so good with all those gadgets, you know. He's studying computer science at the university. It's amazing what they can do with computers these days. When I was taking a few classes at the community college—oh, this must have been back in the late seventies—we had to use typewriters. And if you made a mistake, you had to use that little white correction fluid. It smelled awful, like chemicals, and it took forever to dry. If you typed over it too soon, it would make a terrible mess on the ribbon. I had a lovely pale blue Smith Corona typewriter. My father bought it for me when I graduated high school. He was always so proud of my typing speed. He used to time me with his pocket watch. It was a beautiful gold watch that belonged to his grandfather, passed down through the family. It had this intricate engraving on the back of a train, because his grandfather worked for the railroad. The Union Pacific, I believe it was. They used to travel all across the country...\n\nBut, oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? What was the question? Something about making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say I do that often. Just the usual little slips anyone might have.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life that might be contributing to your current difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? In my whole life? Goodness, no, I've had a very blessed life, really. Nothing I would call traumatic. \n\nI mean, there was the time my sister Evelyn lost her favorite brooch at the state fair. Oh, she was absolutely devastated. It was this beautiful cameo pin our grandmother gave her, with a delicate little gold rim. We searched everywhere for it—the pie-tasting pavilion, the Ferris wheel line, even near those giant pumpkins they give blue ribbons to. Have you ever seen one of those pumpkins? They're practically the size of a Volkswagen! I don't know how they get them that big. Probably some special fertilizer. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, uses coffee grounds on his hydrangeas, and let me tell you, the blooms are as big as dinner plates. A gorgeous shade of blue, too. He says it's the acidity in the coffee. I tried it once with my rose bushes, but I think I used the wrong kind of coffee. Arthur used to buy that cheap instant stuff in the big glass jars. It tasted like hot dirt, if you ask me, but he drank it every single morning with exactly two sugars. Never cream. He said cream ruined the flavor. Flavor! As if that instant powder had any flavor to begin with. We used to keep the empty jars to store nails and screws out in the garage. Arthur had a whole wall of them, all labeled with that little embossing machine that punches the white letters into the plastic tape. Click, click, click... it made such a satisfying sound. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking me? Traumatic events? No, no, nothing like that. Just a very ordinary, happy life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and organizing is something that has been present throughout your life, or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: Throughout my life? Oh, well... yes, I suppose it has always been this way, really. Even when I was a little girl, my teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was a bright student but very careless. They said I was always forgetting my books or leaving my homework at home, and they constantly had to remind me to finish my seatwork before I was allowed to go outside for recess. \n\nRecess was always my favorite part of the day, of course. We used to play hopscotch on the blacktop behind the school. My best friend Sally had this special piece of pink chalk she brought from home, and she'd draw the most perfect, straight lines for the squares. She was very artistic, Sally. She ended up going to art school in Chicago. I always wanted to visit her there, but the train tickets were so expensive back then. And the trains! They were so loud and smoky. I remember taking the train to visit my aunt in St. Louis once, and I wore a brand new white linen dress. By the time we arrived, there were little black soot spots all over the collar. My aunt tried to get them out with lemon juice and sunshine—she swore by lemon juice for everything. She even put it in her hair to make it lighter in the summer. It smelled wonderful, like a fresh pie...\n\nOh, dear, I've done it again, haven't I? What were you asking me? Oh, right, if it's new. No, no, it's been like this since I was in grade school, I'm afraid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It has something to do with the acidity. But yes, I'll just start listening to them, and then I look down at my paper and I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning. It just flies right out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or anxious when you're faced with these tasks and can't seem to focus?\n\nPatient: Overwhelmed? Oh, well... I'm not really sure if I'd call it overwhelmed, exactly. Or anxious. I mean, maybe a little? But mostly it's just... well, my husband, he gets anxious. Oh, terribly anxious, especially when he watches the news. He sits in his recliner—we bought it at that furniture store downtown, the one that used to be a movie theater back in the fifties. We saw *Singin' in the Rain* there on our first date. Such a wonderful movie, they just don't make them like that anymore, with the real dancing. Gene Kelly was just magnificent. \n\nBut yes, he watches the news and gets all worked up about the economy and the price of eggs. Have you seen the price of eggs lately? It's just astonishing. I used to bake an angel food cake every Sunday, takes twelve egg whites, you know. Twelve! But now I just make a simple pound cake instead. It's easier anyway, and it keeps longer in that nice glass dome my sister gave me for our anniversary. Our fortieth, I think it was. Or maybe the forty-fifth? \n\nAnyway... what were we talking about? Oh, feeling anxious. I don't know, I just... I haven't really thought about it that way. I just know I can't seem to get the things done that I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, I suppose... I don't really know if I'd use that word. I just feel so mixed up sometimes. It's more like a fog, really. Like when the weather changes and that thick fog rolls in off the lake. We used to take the kids to the lake every summer, rented a little cabin with a screened-in porch. The mosquitoes were just terrible, though. We'd burn those little green coils, you know the ones? They smelled awful, but they kept the bugs away. My son used to try and catch the fireflies in a mason jar. \n\nBut anxious... I don't think so. I just feel bad that I can't seem to keep things straight anymore. It's just frustrating, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? Oh, goodness, yes. I just can't seem to stay still, even when I really ought to. Like when I'm trying to sit politely at the planning meetings for the senior center. I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot under the table. Or I'll be clicking my pen—click, click, click. It's one of those nice gel pens, you know, the ones that write so smoothly? I bought a whole pack of them at the pharmacy last week. The pharmacist there, David, he is just the sweetest young man. He always asks about our golden retriever, Buster. Buster is getting so gray around the muzzle now, just like my husband! We've had him since he was just a little puppy, fit right in a shoebox. \n\nBut yes, at the meetings, I just shift around in my chair so much that the other ladies actually stop and look over at me. Martha gave me quite the glare last Tuesday because I was moving around so much while she was talking. She's very strict about Robert's Rules of Order, you know. She used to be a high school principal, and she still treats us all like we're teenagers in detention if we make too much noise. But I just can't help it, my body just wants to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're moving around or fidgeting even when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about those meetings, I certainly don't do it on purpose! Goodness no. I'll just be sitting there, trying so hard to be polite and listen, and suddenly I realize my foot is going a mile a minute, or I'm rubbing my hands together again. I don't even know I'm doing it until I catch myself. Or until someone gives me a look, like Martha does. \n\nIt's like my body just has a mind of its own. I was actually thinking about taking up knitting to give my hands something to do when I have to sit still. My grandmother was a beautiful knitter. She made these gorgeous afghans, all in pastel colors. We had this lovely peach-colored one draped over the back of the sofa for years and years. Oh, it was so warm in the winter... the winters used to be so much colder back then, didn't they? We'd get snow piled all the way up to the porch steps. \n\nBut yes, I definitely don't try to fidget. I just sort of... realize I'm shifting around in my chair or messing with my pen after I've already been doing it for heaven knows how long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad? Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Frustrated, certainly, like I mentioned before with the fog and not keeping things straight, but not down in the dumps. I actually try to keep a very cheerful disposition. My mother always used to say, \"A smile is the best umbrella on a rainy day.\" She was such a wise woman, my mother. She used to bake the most wonderful apple pies. The secret, she said, was in the crust—she used half butter and half shortening. And she always put a little sprinkle of nutmeg in with the cinnamon. I tried to make it for Thanksgiving last year, but it just didn't taste the same. We had Thanksgiving at my daughter's house this time. She has this beautiful big dining table that seats twelve. It's solid oak, very heavy. It took three men just to get it through her front door when she bought it! \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel sad. I have my family, I have Buster... I'm just terribly annoyed with myself for being so scattered all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid tasks because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. If it's something where I have to really concentrate... like when there's a form to fill out, or those awful insurance papers to read, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies at the center. I just look at the pile and my head feels completely worn out before I even start! \n\nSo I keep finding little reasons to put it off. I'll tell myself, \"Oh, I really must water the African violets first.\" I have three of them on my kitchen windowsill, you know. The purple one is just blooming beautifully right now. I got the original cutting from my neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, oh, must be fifteen years ago now. She moved down to Florida shortly after that to be closer to her daughter. Tampa, I think? Or maybe it was Boca Raton. Her daughter had just had a baby boy. Goodness, that boy must be driving by now! They grow up so fast, don't they? It feels like just yesterday my own children were running through the sprinklers in the backyard. We used to have one of those yellow slip-and-slides, and the grass would get so muddy... \n\nBut yes, the paperwork and the schedules. I just avoid them like the plague because it feels like climbing a mountain just thinking about starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Details? Oh, I suppose... well, I don't really know if I'd say I have trouble with them. I just don't always notice them, I guess? Like, I'll read a recipe and completely miss that it said \"teaspoon\" instead of \"tablespoon.\" I did that just last week with a batch of bran muffins. Oh, they were terribly salty! I had to throw the whole lot out for the birds. The blue jays didn't seem to mind, though. We have a pair of them that nest in the big oak tree out front every spring. They are so noisy, squawking away at all hours of the morning! Buster, my dog, he just sits at the window and barks back at them. He's a terrier mix, you know, very spunky. We got him from the shelter about five years ago after my husband passed. The man at the shelter said he was a \"schnoodle,\" a schnauzer and poodle mix, but I think he's got some Jack Russell in him, the way he jumps. He can jump right up onto the back of the sofa from a standstill! \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, details. I'm not sure I really have trouble with them, I just... I suppose I gloss over things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid doing things because you worry about not being able to do them correctly?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think it's because I'm worried about doing them wrong. I mean, I know how to do things properly, I've been running a household for nearly fifty years! It's just the... the energy it takes to keep it all straight in my head. But no, not because I'm afraid of making a mistake. I've never been a perfectionist, really. \n\nMy sister, Elaine, now she was a perfectionist. Everything in her house had to be just so. She used to iron her sheets! Can you imagine? Ironing bedsheets! She had this heavy old GE iron, the kind with the thick cord wrapped in that fabric stuff. It weighed a ton. She actually burned a hole right through one of her good pillowcases once because she left it sitting too long while she went to answer the telephone. It was one of those old rotary phones, you know, the black ones that hung on the kitchen wall with the extra-long cord so you could stretch it all the way to the stove while you were cooking. We used to talk for hours on Sundays. The rates were cheaper on Sundays back then. I remember when long distance was a real luxury, you had to wait until after 9 PM to call out of state. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't avoid things because I'm worried about getting them wrong. I'm perfectly capable, it's just getting my brain to cooperate and focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? Oh, heavens no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I have my moments, of course, especially since Arthur passed, but mostly I'm quite content. I have my garden, and the ladies at the center, and Buster keeping me on my toes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of empty, I did open my pantry yesterday and realized I was completely out of peach preserves. I usually make them myself every August when the peaches are in season. We used to drive out to this lovely little orchard off Route 9—Miller's Farm, it was called. Old Mr. Miller had the sweetest peaches you ever tasted. He always wore these faded denim overalls and a straw hat that looked like it had seen better days. He had a golden retriever named Rusty who would follow him everywhere, just trotting along behind the tractor. I wonder if that farm is still there? So many of those family places got bought up by developers to build those big, identical houses. You know the ones, they all have those tiny little yards where you can barely fit a patio set, let alone a proper garden. I couldn't imagine living without a garden. Where would I put my hydrangeas? \n\nBut no, I don't feel sad or empty most of the time. I stay busy enough, even if I don't always get around to the things I'm supposed to be doing!\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Stressful or traumatic? No, no, nothing like that. It's been quite peaceful around here lately, really. Just the usual day-to-day things. \n\nWell, I suppose the most stressful thing recently was when the mail carrier started coming at a different time. It used to be you could set your watch by him—11:15 sharp, every morning. It was a lovely man named Gary, always had a pocket full of those little milk bone treats for Buster. But then Gary retired last month. He moved down to Florida, somewhere near Sarasota, I think. His daughter lives down there, and she just had twins. A boy and a girl! Imagine having twins at her age, she must be nearly forty. My cousin Betty had twins back in '78, and let me tell you, it was a handful. She used to put them in this massive double stroller that took up the entire sidewalk. You couldn't even get past her if you were walking the other way, you'd have to step right off the curb into the street. And the streets back then weren't paved as nicely as they are now, all those cobblestones downtown. I twisted my ankle on one of those cobblestones once, right in front of the old Woolworth's. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, no, nothing traumatic or overly stressful. Just the mail being late!\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Completing tasks? Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say I have trouble finishing things once I actually get my hands on them. I mean, I knitted a whole scarf just last week! I used this lovely periwinkle yarn I found on sale at the craft shop. That shop used to be over by the old cinema, you know, the one that had the big neon marquee with the missing letters? We used to go to the matinees there for fifty cents when I was younger. They always played those wonderful old Hitchcock films. \n\nRear Window was my absolute favorite. Grace Kelly's dresses in that movie were just exquisite. Women really dressed up back then, even just to go to the grocery store. You'd never catch my mother leaving the house without her pearls and a matching handbag. She had this one black patent leather purse that always smelled like peppermint because she kept those little individually wrapped candies at the bottom. The ones with the red and white stripes? They get so sticky if you leave them in there too long. I actually found one in an old coat pocket once and it was practically glued to the lining. It was a terrible shame because it was a beautiful wool coat, a real heavy winter one that Arthur bought me for our fifth anniversary. We went up to the mountains that year, and it snowed three feet... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh! Finishing things. No, if I start a task, I usually get it done just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or having difficulty sustaining attention on tasks?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Or trouble paying attention? Oh, I don't think so. I've always been quite particular about things, really. \n\nAlthough, I suppose I did put the television remote in the refrigerator yesterday. But that's just because I was holding it when the phone rang, and I was trying to get out the milk for my tea at the same time. The phone ring is so loud now, I had my grandson turn it up because I kept missing calls when I was out in the garden. He's so good with all those gadgets, you know. He's studying computer science at the university. It's amazing what they can do with computers these days. When I was taking a few classes at the community college—oh, this must have been back in the late seventies—we had to use typewriters. And if you made a mistake, you had to use that little white correction fluid. It smelled awful, like chemicals, and it took forever to dry. If you typed over it too soon, it would make a terrible mess on the ribbon. I had a lovely pale blue Smith Corona typewriter. My father bought it for me when I graduated high school. He was always so proud of my typing speed. He used to time me with his pocket watch. It was a beautiful gold watch that belonged to his grandfather, passed down through the family. It had this intricate engraving on the back of a train, because his grandfather worked for the railroad. The Union Pacific, I believe it was. They used to travel all across the country...\n\nBut, oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? What was the question? Something about making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say I do that often. Just the usual little slips anyone might have.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your life that might be contributing to your current difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Traumatic events? In my whole life? Goodness, no, I've had a very blessed life, really. Nothing I would call traumatic. \n\nI mean, there was the time my sister Evelyn lost her favorite brooch at the state fair. Oh, she was absolutely devastated. It was this beautiful cameo pin our grandmother gave her, with a delicate little gold rim. We searched everywhere for it—the pie-tasting pavilion, the Ferris wheel line, even near those giant pumpkins they give blue ribbons to. Have you ever seen one of those pumpkins? They're practically the size of a Volkswagen! I don't know how they get them that big. Probably some special fertilizer. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, uses coffee grounds on his hydrangeas, and let me tell you, the blooms are as big as dinner plates. A gorgeous shade of blue, too. He says it's the acidity in the coffee. I tried it once with my rose bushes, but I think I used the wrong kind of coffee. Arthur used to buy that cheap instant stuff in the big glass jars. It tasted like hot dirt, if you ask me, but he drank it every single morning with exactly two sugars. Never cream. He said cream ruined the flavor. Flavor! As if that instant powder had any flavor to begin with. We used to keep the empty jars to store nails and screws out in the garage. Arthur had a whole wall of them, all labeled with that little embossing machine that punches the white letters into the plastic tape. Click, click, click... it made such a satisfying sound. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking me? Traumatic events? No, no, nothing like that. Just a very ordinary, happy life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and organizing is something that has been present throughout your life, or is it a relatively new development?\n\nPatient: Throughout my life? Oh, well... yes, I suppose it has always been this way, really. Even when I was a little girl, my teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was a bright student but very careless. They said I was always forgetting my books or leaving my homework at home, and they constantly had to remind me to finish my seatwork before I was allowed to go outside for recess. \n\nRecess was always my favorite part of the day, of course. We used to play hopscotch on the blacktop behind the school. My best friend Sally had this special piece of pink chalk she brought from home, and she'd draw the most perfect, straight lines for the squares. She was very artistic, Sally. She ended up going to art school in Chicago. I always wanted to visit her there, but the train tickets were so expensive back then. And the trains! They were so loud and smoky. I remember taking the train to visit my aunt in St. Louis once, and I wore a brand new white linen dress. By the time we arrived, there were little black soot spots all over the collar. My aunt tried to get them out with lemon juice and sunshine—she swore by lemon juice for everything. She even put it in her hair to make it lighter in the summer. It smelled wonderful, like a fresh pie...\n\nOh, dear, I've done it again, haven't I? What were you asking me? Oh, right, if it's new. No, no, it's been like this since I was in grade school, I'm afraid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
